#1d07:beam10m:conv_04:plan-1EVIDENCE
score · 13.7204timestamp · July-01-2024
text
 \( \nabla f \) will increase the value of \( f \) most rapidly.

- **Steepest Descent:**
  Moving in the opposite direction of \( \nabla f \) (i.e., \(-\nabla f\)) will decrease the value of \( f \) most rapidly.

### Practical Steps to Master Partial Derivatives

1. **Understand the Basics:**
   - Learn how to compute partial derivatives for various functions.
   - Understand the geometric interpretation of partial derivatives.

2. **Visualize Functions and Gradients:**
   - Use tools like Desmos or MATLAB to plot functions and their gradients.
   - Observe how the gradient vectors behave at different points on the surface.

3. **Practice with Examples:**
   - Work through a variety of problems involving partial derivatives and gradients.
   - Apply the concept of gradients to optimization problems.

4. **Relate to Optimization Algorithms:**
   - Understand how gradient descent and gradient ascent algorithms work.
   - See how the gradient guides the search for optimal points.

### Example in Desmos

To visualize the function and its gradient in Desmos:

1. **Plot the Function:**
   - Enter `z = 5 - x^2 - y^2` to plot the surface.

2. **Plot the Gradient Vectors:**
   - Add vectors at specific points to show the direction of the gradient.
   - For example, at \((1, 1)\), plot the vector `(-2, -2)` starting from \((1, 1)\).

### Summary

The gradient \( \nabla f \) of a function \( f(x, y) \) points in the direction of the steepest ascent. For the function \( f(x, y) = 5 - x^2 - y^2 \), the gradient \( \nabla f = (-2x, -2y) \) always points towards the origin, which is the maximum point of the function.

Mastering partial derivatives involves understanding how to compute them, visualizing their effects, and applying them to optimization problems. By practicing with various functions and visualizing their gradients, you can build a strong foundation in this area.

Would you like to go through another example or need further clarification on any part of the explanation?

### Quick Steps to Plot in Desmos

1. **Plot the Surface:**
   - Enter `z = 5 - x^2 - y^2` to plot the surface of the function.

2. **Plot Gradient Vectors:**
   - To visualize the gradient vectors, you can plot a few vectors manually. For example, at the point \((1, 1)\), the gradient vector is \((-2, -2)\).
   - You can plot this vector by adding a parametric line in Desmos:
     - Enter `x_1 = 1 + t * (-2)` and `y_1 = 1 + t * (-2)` for \(0 \leq t \leq 1\).

3. **Add More Vectors:**
   - Repeat the process for other points to see how the gradient vectors behave. For example, at \((-1, -1)\), the gradient vector is \((2, 2)\):
     - Enter `x_2 = -1 + t * 2` and `y_2 = -1 + t * 2` for \(0 \leq t \leq 1\).

### Example in Desmos

Here's a quick example of how you might set this up in Desmos:

1. **Surface Plot:**
   - Enter `z = 5 - x^2 - y^2`.

2. **Gradient Vectors:**
   - For the point \((1, 1)\):
     - Enter `x_1 = 1 + t * (-2)` and `y_1 = 1 + t * (-2)` for \(0 \leq t \leq 1\).
   - For the point \((-1, -1)\):
     - Enter `x_2 = -1 + t * 2` and `y_2 = -1 + t * 2` for \(0 \leq t \leq 1\).

### Additional Tips

- **Interactive Exploration:**
  - Use sliders to dynamically change the points and observe how the gradient vectors change.
  - For example, you can define a point \((a, b)\) and plot the gradient vector at that point.

- **Visualizing Contours:**
  - Plot contour lines of the function to see how the gradient vectors align with the contours.

### Summary

By plotting the function and its gradient vectors in Desmos, you can visually confirm that the gradient points in the direction of the steepest ascent, which is towards the origin for the function \( f(x, y) = 5 - x^2 - y^2 \).

If you have any more questions or need further assistance, feel free to ask! Enjoy exploring the function and its gradients.

## Assistant

Great job on achieving a milestone by solving 15 partial derivative problems with 93% accuracy! Maintaining a high level of accuracy as you transition to PDE core study requires consistent practice, review, and strategic planning. Here are some steps to help you maintain 90%+ accuracy across all prerequisite topics:

### 1. **Regular Review and Practice**

- **Daily Practice:** Dedicate a portion of your study time each day to reviewing and solving problems from prerequisite topics.
- **Mixed Practice:** Include a mix of problems from different topics to ensure you maintain proficiency across all areas.

### 2. **Target Weak Areas**

- **Identify Weaknesses:** Regularly assess your performance and identify any weak areas or topics where you consistently make mistakes.
- **Focused Practice:** Spend extra time on these weak areas. Solve additional problems and seek out more challenging questions to build confidence.

### 3. **Use a Variety of Resources**

- **Textbooks and Notes:** Continue to use your textbooks and class notes for theory and examples.
- **Online Resources:** Utilize online platforms like Khan Academy, MIT OpenCourseWare, and Paul's Online Math Notes for additional practice and explanations.
- **Practice Problems:** Use problem sets from previous exams, homework assignments, and supplementary materials.

### 4. **Seek Feedback and Collaboration**

- **Peer Discussions:** Engage in discussions with peers to gain different perspectives and insights.
- **Instructor Feedback:** Regularly consult with your instructor or teaching assistant to get feedback on your work and clarify any misunderstandings.

### 5. **Simulate Exam Conditions**

- **Timed Practice:** Occasionally, simulate exam conditions by timing yourself and working through problems without notes or resources.
- **Review Mistakes:** After each practice session, review any mistakes carefully to understand where you went wrong and how to avoid similar errors in the future.

### 6. **Maintain a Log and Checklist**

- **Log of Problems:** Keep a log of the problems you've solved, noting any difficulties or insights gained.
- **Checklist:** Create a checklist of the prerequisite topics and mark them off as you review and practice them.

### 7. **Stay Organized and Track Progress**

- **Weekly Reviews:** At the end of each week, review all the problems you've solved and identify any recurring mistakes or weak areas.
- **Adjust Study Plan:** Modify your study plan based on your progress and any identified weaknesses.

### 8. **Stay Motivated and Positive**

- **Set Milestones:** Break your study goals into smaller milestones and celebrate small victories.
- **Positive Mindset:** Maintain a positive mindset and believe in your ability to succeed. Remind yourself of your progress and achievements regularly.

### Example Study Plan

Here's an example of how you might structure your study plan:

- **Day 1:** Solve 5 problems on multivariable calculus.
- **Day 2:** Solve 5 problems on linear algebra.
- **Day 3:** Solve 5 problems on ODEs.
- **Day 4:** Solve 5 problems on vector calculus.
- **Day 5:** Review and correct any mistakes from Days 1-4.
- **Day 6:** Solve 5 more problems on multivariable calculus.
- **Day 7:** Solve 5 more problems on linear algebra.
- **Day 8:** Solve 5 more problems on ODEs.
- **Day 9:** Solve 5 more problems on vector calculus.
- **Day 10:** Comprehensive review and final corrections.

### Summary

By following these steps, you can maintain a high level of accuracy as you transition to PDE core study. Regular practice, targeted review of weak areas, and strategic use of resources will help you stay proficient across all prerequisite topics.

I'm trying to decompose the double integral ∫∫(2x²+y)dA over the region [0,1]x[0,3] into smaller steps to get the result of 7, and I want to make sure I can do it within 15 minutes, can you help me break it down and verify my steps? ->-> 10,19

## Assistant

Certainly! Let's break down the double integral \(\iint_{[0,1] \times [0,3]} (2x^2 + y) \, dA\) into smaller steps and verify each step to ensure you can complete it within 15 minutes.

### Step-by-Step Breakdown

1. **Set Up the Double Integral:**
   The region of integration is \([0,1] \times [0,3]\). We can write the double integral as:
   \[
   \iint_{[0,1] \times [0,3]} (2x^2 + y) \, dA = \int_0^1 \int_0^3 (2x^2 + y) \, dy \, dx
   \]

2. **Integrate with Respect to \( y \):**
   First, integrate the inner integral with respect to \( y \):
   \[
   \int_0^3 (2x^2 + y) \, dy
   \]
   - Treat \( x \) as a constant since we are integrating with respect to \( y \).
   - Integrate term by term:
     \[
     \int_0^3 2x^2 \, dy + \int_0^3 y \, dy
     \]
   - The first term is:
     \[
     2x^2 \int_0^3 1 \, dy = 2x^2 [y]_0^3 = 2x^2 (3 - 0) = 6x^2
     \]
   - The second term is:
     \[
     \int_0^3 y \, dy = \left[ \frac{y^2}{2} \right]_0^3 = \frac{3^2}{2} - \frac{0^2}{2} = \frac{9}{2}
     \]
   - Combine the results:
     \[
     \int_0^3 (2x^2 + y) \, dy = 6x^2 + \frac{9}{2}
     \]

3. **Integrate with Respect to \( x \):**
   Now, integrate the result with respect to \( x \):
   \[
   \int_0^1 \left( 6x^2 + \frac{9}{2} \right) \, dx
   \]
   - Integrate term by term:
     \[
     \int_0^1 6x^2 \, dx + \int_0^1 \frac{9}{2} \,
#2d07:beam10m:conv_04:plan-2DISTRACTOR

I've been trying to follow the method, but I'm only at 60% proficiency, and I want to make sure I'm using it correctly to improve my note-taking skills. ->-> 5,5

## Assistant

Certainly! The summary section of the Cornell method is a crucial component for consolidating your understanding and retaining key information. Here's how you can create an effective summary, along with an example for the method of separation of variables in PDEs.

### How to Create a Good Summary

1. **Identify Key Points:**
   - Review your notes and identify the main ideas, definitions, and procedures.
   - Highlight the most important concepts and steps.

2. **Condense Information:**
   - Summarize the key points in a concise manner.
   - Use bullet points or short sentences to capture the essence of the topic.

3. **Reflect on Connections:**
   - Think about how the key points relate to each other and to broader concepts.
   - Include any insights or connections you have made during your study.

4. **Use Your Own Words:**
   - Write the summary in your own words to ensure you fully understand the material.
   - This helps reinforce your comprehension and retention.

5. **Keep It Brief:**
   - Aim for a summary that is no more than a few sentences or bullet points.
   - The goal is to capture the core ideas succinctly.

### Example: Method of Separation of Variables

#### Notes Column (Right Side)

- **Definition:** The method of separation of variables is a technique used to solve partial differential equations by assuming a solution that is a product of functions of independent variables.
- **General Form:** Assume \( u(x, t) = X(x)T(t) \).
- **Steps:**
  1. Substitute \( u(x, t) = X(x)T(t) \) into the PDE.
  2. Separate the variables to form ordinary differential equations (ODEs).
  3. Solve the resulting ODEs.
  4. Combine the solutions to form the general solution.
- **Example:** Consider the heat equation \( \frac{\partial u}{\partial t} = k \frac{\partial^2 u}{\partial x^2} \).
  - Assume \( u(x, t) = X(x)T(t) \).
  - Substitute and separate variables to get \( \frac{T'}{kT} = \frac{X''}{X} = -\lambda \).
  - Solve the ODEs \( T' + k\lambda T = 0 \) and \( X'' + \lambda X = 0 \).
  - Combine solutions to form \( u(x, t) = \sum_{n=1}^\infty A_n e^{-k\lambda_n t} \sin(\sqrt{\lambda_n} x) \).

#### Cues Column (Left Side)

- **Separation of Variables Definition**
- **General Form**
- **Steps**
- **Example: Heat Equation**

#### Summary Section (Bottom)

- **Summary:** The method of separation of variables is a technique for solving PDEs by assuming a solution of the form \( u(x, t) = X(x)T(t) \). This leads to ODEs for \( X(x) \) and \( T(t) \), which can be solved separately.      - Enter `x_1 = 1 + t * (-2)` and `y_1 = 1 + t * (-2)` for \(0 \leq t \leq 1\).
   - For the point \((-1, -1)\):
     - Enter `x_2 = -1 + t * 2` and `y_2 = -1 + t * 2` for \(0 \leq t \leq 1\).

I'm trying to decompose the double integral ∫∫(2x²+y)dA over the region [0,1]x[0,3] into smaller steps to get the result of 7, and I want to make sure I can do it within 15 minutes, can you help me break it down and verify my steps? ->-> 10,19

## Assistant

Certainly! Let's break down the double integral \(\iint_{[0,1] \times [0,3]} (2x^2 + y) \, dA\) into smaller steps and verify each step to ensure you can complete it within 15 minutes.

### Step-by-Step Breakdown

1. **Set Up the Double Integral:**
   The region of integration is \([0,1] \times [0,3]\). We can write the double integral as:
   \[
   \iint_{[0,1] \times [0,3]} (2x^2 + y) \, dA = \int_0^1 \int_0^3 (2x^2 + y) \, dy \, dx
   \]

2. **Integrate with Respect to \( y \):**
   First, integrate the inner integral with respect to \( y \):
   \[
   \int_0^3 (2x^2 + y) \, dy
   \]
   - Treat \( x \) as a constant since we are integrating with respect to \( y \).
   - Integrate term by term:
     \[
     \int_0^3 2x^2 \, dy + \int_0^3 y \, dy
     \]
   - The first term is:
     \[
     2x^2 \int_0^3 1 \, dy = 2x^2 [y]_0^3 = 2x^2 (3 - 0) = 6x^2
     \]
   - The second term is:
     \[
     \int_0^3 y \, dy = \left[ \frac{y^2}{2} \right]_0^3 = \frac{3^2}{2} - \frac{0^2}{2} = \frac{9}{2}
     \]
   - Combine the results:
     \[
     \int_0^3 (2x^2 + y) \, dy = 6x^2 + \frac{9}{2}
     \]

3. **Integrate with Respect to \( x \):**
   Now, integrate the result with respect to \( x \):
   \[
   \int_0^1 \left( 6x^2 + \frac{9}{2} \right) \, dx
   \]
   - Integrate term by term:
     \[
     \int_0^1 6x^2 \, dx + \int_0^1 \frac{9}{2} \,

### How to Create a Good Summary

1. **Identify Key Points:**
   - Review your notes and identify the main ideas, definitions, and procedures.
   - Highlight the most important concepts and steps.

   - Summarize the key points in a concise manner.

   - Aim for a summary that is no more than a few sentences or bullet points.
   - The goal is to capture the core ideas succinctly.

### Example: Method of Separation of Variables

#### Notes Column (Right Side)

- **Definition:** The method of separation of variables is a technique used to solve partial differential equations by assuming a solution that is a product of functions of independent variables.
- **General Form:** Assume \( u(x, t) = X(x)T(t) \).
- **Steps:**
  1. Substitute \( u(x, t) = X(x)T(t) \) into the PDE.
  2. Separate the variables to form ordinary differential equations (ODEs).
  3. Solve the resulting ODEs.
  4. Combine the solutions to form the general solution.
- **Example:** Consider the heat equation \( \frac{\partial u}{\partial t} = k \frac{\partial^2 u}{\partial x^2} \).
  - Assume \( u(x, t) = X(x)T(t) \).
  - Substitute and separate variables to get \( \frac{T'}{kT} = \frac{X''}{X} = -\lambda \).
  - Solve the ODEs \( T' + k\lambda T = 0 \) and \( X'' + \lambda X = 0 \).
  - Combine solutions to form \( u(x, t) = \sum_{n=1}^\infty A_n e^{-k\lambda_n t} \sin(\sqrt{\lambda_n} x) \).

- **Separation of Variables Definition**
- **General Form**

Section 14.1 with at least 90% accuracy? ->-> 2,18\n\n## Assistant\n\nCertainly! Let's delve into the concept of optimization and how it relates to gradients, and then provide an example to illustrate these ideas. Finally, we'll discuss how this knowledge can help you achieve your goal of mastering partial derivatives.\n\n### Concept of Optimization and Gradients\n\nThe gradient of a function \\( f(x, y) \\), denoted by \\( \\nabla f \\), is a vector that points in the direction of the steepest ascent of the function. This is because the gradient is defined as:\n\n\\[\n\\nabla f = \\left( \\frac{\\partial f}{\\partial x}, \\frac{\\partial f}{\\partial y} \\right)\n\\]\n\nThe magnitude of the gradient vector represents the rate of change of the function in the direction of the gradient. Therefore, the direction of the gradient vector is the direction in which the function increases most rapidly.\n\n### Example: \\( f(x, y) = 5 - x^2 - y^2 \\)\n\nLet's consider the function \\( f(x, y) = 5 - x^2 - y^2 \\).\n\n1. **Compute the partial derivatives:**\n\n   - Partial derivative with respect to \\( x \\):\n     \\[\n     \\frac{\\partial f}{\\partial x} = -2x\n     \\]\n\n   - Partial derivative with respect to \\( y \\):\n     \\[\n     \\frac{\\partial f}{\\partial y} = -2y\n     \\]\n\n2. **Form the gradient vector:**\n\n   \\[\n   \\nabla f = (-2x, -2y)\n   \\]\n\n3. **Find the critical points:**\n\n   Set the gradient vector to zero to find the critical points:\n   \\[\n   \\nabla f = (-2x, -2y) = (0, 0)\n   \\]\n\n   This gives us the system of equations:\n   \\[\n   -2x = 0 \\implies x = 0\n   \\]\n   \\[\n   -2y = 0 \\implies y = 0\n   \\]\n\n   So, the critical point is \\( (0, 0) \\).\n\n4. **Evaluate the function at the critical point:**\n\n   \\[\n   f(0, 0) = 5 - 0^2 - 0^2 = 5\n   \\]\n\n5. **Determine the nature of the critical point:**\n\n   To determine whether this critical point is a maximum, minimum, or saddle point, we can use the second partial derivatives and the Hessian matrix. The Hessian matrix \\( H \\) is given by:\n   \\[\n   H = \\begin{pmatrix}\n   \\frac{\\partial^2 f}{\\partial x^2} & \\frac{\\partial^2 f}{\\partial x \\partial y} \\\\\n   \\frac{\\partial^2 f}{\\partial y \\partial x} & \\frac{\\partial^2 f}{\\partial y^2}\n   \\end{pmatrix}\n   \\]\n\n   Compute the second partial derivatives:\n   \\[\n   \\frac{\\partial^2 f}{\\partial x^2} = -2\n   \\]\n   \\[\n   \\frac{\\partial^2 f}{\\partial y^2} = -2\n   \\]\n   \\[\n   \\frac{\\partial^2 f}{\\partial x \\partial y} = 0\n   \\]\n   \\[\n   \\frac{\\partial^2 f}{\\partial y \\partial x} = 0\n   \\]\n\n   So, the Hessian matrix is:\n   \\[\n   H = \\begin{pmatrix}\n   -2 & 0 \\\\\n   0 & -2\n   \\end{pmatrix}\n   \\]\n\n   The determinant of the Hessian matrix is:\n   \\[\n   \\det(H) = (-2)(-2) - (0)(0) = 4\n   \\]\n\n   Since the determinant is positive and the leading principal minor (the top-left element) is negative, the Hessian is negative definite. This indicates that the critical point \\( (0, 0) \\) is a local maximum.\n\n### Application to Calculus and Analysis\n\nUnderstanding gradients and optimization is crucial for solving problems in calculus and analysis. Here are some ways this knowledge can help you achieve your goal of mastering partial derivatives:\n\n1. **Practice Regularly:** Solve a variety of problems involving partial derivatives, gradients, and optimization. Let's delve into the concept of gradients and how they relate to optimization, specifically using the function \\( f(x, y) = 5 - x^2 - y^2 \\).\n\n### Concept of Gradients\n\nThe gradient of a function \\( f(x, y) \\) is a vector that points in the direction of the greatest rate of increase of the function. It is denoted by \\( \\nabla f \\) and is defined as:\n\n\\[\n\\nabla f = \\left( \\frac{\\partial f}{\\partial x}, \\frac{\\partial f}{\\partial y} \\right)\n\\]\n\nFor the function \\( f(x, y) = 5 - x^2 - y^2 \\):\n\n1. **Compute the Partial Derivatives:**\n   \\[\n   \\frac{\\partial f}{\\partial x} = -2x\n   \\]\n   \\[\n   \\frac{\\partial f}{\\partial y} = -2y\n   \\]\n\n2. **Form the Gradient Vector:**\n   \\[\n   \\nabla f = (-2x, -2y)\n   \\]\n\n### Visualization of Gradients\n\nTo visualize how \\( \\nabla f \\) points to the steepest ascent, let's consider the function \\( f(x, y) = 5 - x^2 - y^2 \\):\n\n- **Function Shape:**\n  The function \\( f(x, y) = 5 - x^2 - y^2 \\) describes a paraboloid that opens downward, with its maximum value at the origin \\((0, 0)\\).\n\n- **Gradient at Different Points:**\n  - At the origin \\((0, 0)\\):\n    \\[\n    \\nabla f(0, 0) = (-2 \\cdot 0, -2 \\cdot 0) = (0, 0)\n    \\]\n    The gradient is zero, indicating that this is a critical point (maximum in this case).\n\n  - At a point \\((1, 1)\\):\n    \\[\n    \\nabla f(1, 1) = (-2 \\cdot 1, -2 \\cdot 1) = (-2, -2)\n    \\]\n    The gradient vector \\((-2, -2)\\) points towards the origin, which is the direction of steepest ascent.\n\n  - At a point \\((-1, -1)\\):\n    \\[\n    \\nabla f(-1, -1) = (-2 \\cdot (-1), -2 \\cdot (-1)) = (2, 2)\n    \\]\n    The gradient vector \\((2, 2)\\) also points towards the origin.\n\n### Connecting to Optimization\n\nIn optimization, the gradient is used to find the maximum or minimum of a function. For a function \\( f(x, y) \\):\n\n- **Steepest Ascent:**\n  The gradient \\( \\nabla f \\) at any point \\((x, y)\\) points in the direction of the steepest ascent. Moving in the direction of \\( \\nabla f \\) will increase the value of \\( f \\) most rapidly.\n\n- **Steepest Descent:**\n  Moving i\n========== daily/d07_beam10m_scale_group_03_1m/d07_beam10m_conv_04_plan-1.md:65739-65994 [score=11.7237] ==========\n of Equation:**\n   - Recognize that \\( \\frac{d^2y}{dx^2} - 4y = 0 \\) is a second-order linear homogeneous differential equation with constant coefficients.\n\n2. **Form the Characteristic Equation:**\n   - Write the characteristic equation \\( r^2 - 4 = 0 \\).\n\n3. **Solve the Characteristic Equation:**\n   - Solve \\( r^2 - 4 = 0 \\) to find the roots \\( r = \\pm 2 \\).\n\n4. **Construct the General Solution:**\n   - Use the roots to write the general solution \\( y(x) = Ae^{2x} + Be^{-2x} \\).\n\n5. **Verify the Solution:**\n   - Double-check each step to ensure accuracy.\n\nThe 20-minute estimate includes time for understanding the problem, performing calculations, and verifying the solution. Understanding the continuity of functions is indeed crucial for Partial Differential Equations (PDEs). Let's break down why the function \\( f(x, y) = \\sin(xy) \\) is continuous everywhere.\n\n### Continuity of Functions\n\nA function \\( f(x, y) \\) is continuous at a point \\((a, b)\\) if:\n\n1. \\( f(a, b) \\) exists.\n2. \\(\\lim_{(x, y) \\to (a, b)} f(x, y)\\) exists.\n3. \\(\\lim_{(x, y) \\to (a, b)} f(x, y) = f(a, b)\\).\n\n### Analyzing \\( f(x, y) = \\sin(xy) \\)\n\n1. **Existence of \\( f(x, y) \\):**\n   - The function \\( \\sin(xy) \\) is defined for all real numbers \\( x \\) and \\( y \\). Therefore, \\( f(x, y) \\) exists for all \\((x, y) \\in \\mathbb{R}^2\\).\n\n2. **Existence of the Limit:**\n   - The sine function, \\( \\sin(z) \\), is continuous for all real numbers \\( z \\). This means that for any sequence \\((x_n, y_n)\\) approaching \\((a, b)\\), the limit \\(\\lim_{n \\to \\infty} \\sin(x_n y_n)\\) exists and equals \\(\\sin(ab)\\).\n\n3. **Equality of the Limit and the Function Value:**\n   - Since \\( \\sin(z) \\) is continuous, we have:\n     \\[\n     \\lim_{(x, y) \\to (a, b)} \\sin(xy) = \\sin\\left(\\lim_{(x, y) \\to (a, b)} xy\\right) = \\sin(ab).\n     \\]\n   - Therefore, \\(\\lim_{(x, y) \\to (a, b)} \\sin(xy) = \\sin(ab) = f(a, b)\\).\n\n### Conclusion\n\nSince \\( f(x, y) = \\sin(xy) \\) satisfies all three conditions of continuity at every point \\((a, b) \\in \\mathbb{R}^2\\), we conclude that \\( f(x, y) = \\sin(xy) \\) is continuous everywhere.\n\n### Summary\n\n- **Existence:** \\( \\sin(xy) \\) is defined for all \\( (x, y) \\).\n- **Limit Existence:** The limit \\(\\lim_{(x, y) \\to (a, b)} \\sin(xy)\\) exists and equals \\(\\sin(ab)\\).\n- **Equality:** The limit equals the function value at \\((a, b)\\).\n\nThus, \\( f(x, y) = \\sin(xy) \\) is continuous everywhere.\n\n### Further Practice\n\nTo solidify your understanding of continuity in multivariable functions, consider the following steps:\n- **Practice More Problems:** Solve additional problems involving continuity of functions.\n- **Review Definitions:** Ensure you understand the formal definition of continuity and how it applies to multivariable functions.\n- **Visualize Functions:** Use graphing tools to visualize functions and their behavior around different points.\n\nBy consistently practicing and reviewing these concepts, you will build a strong foundation in understanding continuity, which is essential for PDEs. Let's break down the problem and the concepts involved step by step.\n\n### Problem: Finding the Span of Vectors in \\(\\mathbb{R}^2\\)\n\nYou need to find the span of the vectors \\([1, 0]\\) and \\([0, 1]\\) in \\(\\mathbb{R}^2\\).\n\n### Step 1: Understanding Span\nThe span of a set of vectors is the set of all possible linear combinations of those vectors. For vectors \\(\\mathbf{v}_1, \\mathbf{v}_2, \\ldots, \\mathbf{v}_n\\), the span is given by:\n\\[\n\\text{Span}(\\mathbf{v}_1, \\mathbf{v}_2, \\ldots, \\mathbf{v}_n) = \\left\\{ c_1 \\mathbf{v}_1 + c_2 \\mathbf{v}_2 + \\cdots + c_n \\mathbf{v}_n \\mid c_1, c_2, \\ldots, c_n \\in \\mathbb{R} \\right\\}\n\\]\n\n### Step 2: Applying the Concept to Your Vectors\nFor the vectors \\(\\mathbf{v}_1 = [1, 0]\\) and \\(\\mathbf{v}_2 = [0, 1]\\), the span is:\n\\[\n\\text{Span}([1, 0], [0, 1]) = \\left\\{ c_1 [1, 0] + c_2 [0, 1] \\mid c_1, c_2 \\in \\mathbb{R} \\right\\}\n\\]\n\n### Step 3: Expressing Any Vector in \\(\\mathbb{R}^2\\)\nAny vector \\(\\mathbf{v} = [a, b]\\) in \\(\\mathbb{R}^2\\) can be written as:\n\\[\n[a, b] = a[1, 0] + b[0, 1]\n\\]\n\nThis shows that any vector in \\(\\mathbb{R}^2\\) can be expressed as a linear combination of \\([1, 0]\\) and \\([0, 1]\\). Therefore, the span of \\([1, 0]\\) and \\([0, 1]\\) is the entire \\(\\mathbb{R}^2\\).\n\n### Final Answer\nThe span of the vectors \\([1, 0]\\) and \\([0, 1]\\) in \\(\\mathbb{R}^2\\) is:\n\\[\n\\boxed{\\mathbb{R}^2}\n\\]\n\n### Understanding Orthogonality\nOrthogonality is a property of vectors where the dot product of two vectors is zero. For vectors \\(\\mathbf{u} = [u_1, u_2]\\) and \\(\\mathbf{v} = [v_1, v_2]\\), the dot product is given by:\n\\[\n\\mathbf{u} \\cdot \\mathbf{v} = u_1 v_1 + u_2 v_2\n\\]\n\nFor the vectors \\([1, 1]\\) and \\([1, -1]\\):\n\\[\n[1, 1] \\cdot [1, -1] = 1 \\cdot 1 + 1 \\cdot (-1) = 1 - 1 = 0\n\\]\n\nSince the dot product is zero, the vectors \\([1, 1]\\) and \\([1, -1]\\) are indeed orthogonal.\n\n### Summary\n- The span of \\([1, 0]\\) and \\([0, 1]\\) is \\(\\mathbb{R}^2\\).\n- Vectors \\([1, 1]\\) and \\([1, -1]\\) are orthogonal because their dot product is zero.\n\nIf you have any more questions or need further examples, feel free to ask!\n\n## User\n\nI've been trying to solve this problem, but I'm having trouble, can you check my work? I've been studying for my PDE prep and I want to make sure I understand the basics, especially after practicing 14 integration problems from Section 15.1 and getting 12 correct, and I've been focusing on setting learning goals based on assessments, like achieving 92% accuracy on 30 ODE problems from Chapter 2 ->-> 7,7\n\n## Assistant\n\nCertainly! Let's start with a basic explanation of integration and then discuss how it fits into your broader study goals for PDEs and ODEs.\n\n### What is Integration?\n\nIntegration is a fundamental concept in calculus that deals with the accumulation of quantities. It is essentially the reverse process of differentiation and can be thought of as finding the area under a curve or the total accumulation of a quantity over a range.\n\nThere are two main types of integrals:\n\n1. **Indefinite Integral**: This is the antiderivative of a function. It represents a family of functions whose derivatives are the original function. It is written as:\n   \\[\n   \\int f(x) \\, dx = F(x) + C\n   \\]\n   where \\( F(x) \\) is an antiderivative of \\( f(x) \\) and \\( C \\) is the constant of integration.\n\n2. **Definite Integral**: This calculates the net area between the function and the x-axis over a specified interval \\([a, b]\\). It is written as:\n   \\[\n   \\int_a^b f(x) \\, dx\n   \\]\n   and it yields a specific number.\n\n### Basic Properties of Integration\n\n- **Linearity**: The integral of a sum of functions is the sum of the integrals of the functions.\n  \\[\n  \\int (f(x) + g(x)) \\, dx = \\int f(x) \\, dx + \\int g(x) \\, dx\n  \\]\n\n- **Constant Multiple Rule**: A constant factor can be moved outside the integral.\n  \\[\n  \\int k f(x) \\, dx = k \\int f(x) \\, dx\n  \\]\n\n### Techniques of Integration\n\n- **Substitution (u-substitution)**: This method is used to simplify the integrand by changing variables.\n  \\[\n  \\int f(g(x)) g'(x) \\, dx = \\int f(u) \\, du \\quad \\text{where} \\quad u = g(x)\n  \\]\n\n- **Integration by Parts**: This method is derived from the product rule of differentiation.\n  \\[\n  \\int u \\, dv = uv - \\int v \\, du\n  \\]\n\n- **Partial Fractions**: This technique is used to decompose rational functions into simpler fractions that can be integrated easily.\n\n### Application in PDEs and ODEs\n\nUnderstanding integration is crucial for solving differential equations, whether they are ordinary (ODEs) or partial (PDEs). Here are a few ways integration is used:\n\n- **Solving ODEs**: Many ODEs can be solved by integrating both sides of the equation. For example, the first-order linear ODE \\( \\frac{dy}{dx} + P(x)y = Q(x) \\) can be solved using an integrating factor.\n\n- **Solving PDEs**: Integration plays a key role in solving PDEs, particularly in methods like separation of variables, where you often need to integrate to find the solution.\n\n### Setting Learning Goals Based on Assessments\n\nGiven your goal to achieve 92% accuracy on 30 ODE problems from Chapter 2, here are some steps to help you reach that goal:\n\n1. **Identify Weak Areas**: Review the problems you got wrong and understand why. Are there specific techniques or concepts you're struggling with?\n\n2. **Focused Practice**: Spend extra time practicing those specific areas. For example, if you're having trouble with integration by parts, find more problems that focus on that technique.\n\n3. **Regular Review**: Regularly review the material to reinforce your understanding. This can include reworking problems you previously solved and reviewing key concepts.\n\n4. **Seek Help**: If you're consistently struggling with certain types of problems, consider seeking help from a tutor or discussing the problems with classmates.\n\n5. **Set Milestones**: Break your larger goal into smaller, achievable milestones. For example, aim to solve 10 problems correctly in a week and gradually increase the difficulty.\n\n### Example Problem\n\nLet's solve a simple integration problem to illustrate the process:\n\n**Problem**: Evaluate the definite integral \\(\\int_0^1 3x^2 \\, dx\\).\n\n**Solution**:\n1. Find the antiderivative of \\(3x^2\\):\n   \\[\n   \\int 3x^2 \\, dx = x^3 + C\n   \\]\n\n2. Let's walk through the process of finding the eigenvalues and eigenvectors for the matrix \\( A = \\begin{bmatrix} 1 & 2 \\\\ 2 & 1 \\end{bmatrix} \\).\n\n### Step 1: Find the Eigenvalues\n\nEigenvalues (\\(\\lambda\\)) are found by solving the characteristic equation of the matrix \\( A \\). The characteristic equation is given by:\n\n\\[\n\\det(A - \\lambda I) = 0\n\\]\n\nwhere \\( I \\) is the identity matrix of the same size as \\( A \\).\n\nFor our matrix \\( A \\):\n\n\\[\nA - \\lambda I = \\begin{bmatrix} 1 & 2 \\\\ 2 & 1 \\end{bmatrix} - \\lambda \\begin{bmatrix} 1 & 0 \\\\  0 & 1 \\end{bmatrix} = \\begin{bmatrix} 1 - \\lambda & 2 \\\\ 2 & 1 - \\lambda \\end{bmatrix}\n\\]\n\nNext, we need to find the determinant of this matrix:\n\n\\[\n\\det(A - \\lambda I) = \\det \\begin{bmatrix} 1 - \\lambda & 2 \\\\ 2 & 1 - \\lambda \\end{bmatrix}\n\\]\n\nThe determinant of a 2x2 matrix \\(\\begin{bmatrix} a & b \\\\ c & d \\end{bmatrix}\\) is calculated as \\( ad - bc \\). So,\n\n\\[\n\\det \\begin{bmatrix} 1 - \\lambda & 2 \\\\ 2 & 1 - \\lambda \\end{bmatrix} = (1 - \\lambda)(1 - \\lambda) - (2)(2)\n\\]\n\nSimplify the expression:\n\n\\[\n(1 - \\lambda)^2 - 4 = 0\n\\]\n\nExpanding and simplifying further:\n\n\\[\n1 - 2\\lambda + \\lambda^2 - 4 = 0\n\\]\n\n\\[\n\\lambda^2 - 2\\lambda - 3 = 0\n\\]\n\nThis is a quadratic equation. To solve for \\(\\lambda\\), we use the quadratic formula:\n\n\\[\n\\lambda = \\frac{-b \\pm \\sqrt{b^2 - 4ac}}{2a}\n\\]\n\nHere, \\( a = 1 \\), \\( b = -2 \\), and \\( c = -3 \\). Plugging these values into the quadratic formula:\n\n\\[\n\\lambda = \\frac{-(-2) \\pm \\sqrt{(-2)^2 - 4(1)(-3)}}{2(1)}\n\\]\n\n\\[\n\\lambda = \\frac{2 \\pm \\sqrt{4 + 12}}{2}\n\\]\n\n\\[\n\\lambda = \\frac{2 \\pm \\sqrt{16}}{2}\n\\]\n\n\\[\n\\lambda = \\frac{2 \\pm 4}{2}\n\\]\n\nThis gives us two solutions:\n\n\\[\n\\lambda_1 = \\frac{2 + 4}{2} = 3\n\\]\n\n\\[\n\\lambda_2 = \\frac{2 - 4}{2} = -1\n\\]\n\nSo, the eigenvalues are \\(\\lambda_1 = 3\\) and \\(\\lambda_2 = -1\\).\n\n### Step 2: Find the Eigenvectors\n\nFor each eigenvalue, we find the corresponding eigenvector by solving the equation:\n\n\\[\n(A - 3 \\end{bmatrix} = \\begin{bmatrix} -2 & 2 \\\\ 2 & -2 \\end{bmatrix}\n\\]\n\nWe need to solve:\n\n\\[\n\\begin{bmatrix} -2 & 2 \\\\ 2 & -2 \\end{bmatrix} \\begin{bmatrix} x \\\\ y \\end{bmatrix} = \\begin{bmatrix} 0 \\\\ 0 \\end{bmatrix}\n\\]\n\nThis gives us the system of equations:\n\n\\[\n-2x + 2y = 0\n\\]\n\n\\[\n2x - 2y = 0\n\\]\n\nBoth equations simplify to:\n\n\\[\nx = y\n\\]\n\nSo, the eigenvector corresponding to \\(\\lambda_1 = 3\\) can be written as:\n\n\\[\n\\mathbf{v}_1 = \\begin{bmatrix} x \\\\ x \\end{bmatrix} = x \\begin{bmatrix} 1 \\\\ 1 \\end{bmatrix}\n\\]\n\nWe can choose \\( x = 1 \\) for simplicity, giving us:\n\n\\[\n\\mathbf{v}_1 = \\begin{bmatrix} 1 \\\\ 1 \\end{bmatrix}\n\\]\n\n#### Eigenvector for \\(\\lambda_2 = -1\\)\n\nSubstitute \\(\\lambda = -1\\) into \\( A - I've been practicing for 20 minutes, but I'm still unsure ->-> 8,6\n\n## Assistant\n\nCertainly! Let's clarify the differences between \\(\\frac{\\partial f}{\\partial x}\\) and \\(\\nabla f\\) and how to use them correctly in different contexts.\n\n### Partial Derivative \\(\\frac{\\partial f}{\\partial x}\\)\n\nThe symbol \\(\\frac{\\partial f}{\\partial x}\\) denotes the partial derivative of a function \\( f \\) with respect to the variable \\( x \\). This is used when \\( f \\) is a function of multiple variables, say \\( f(x, y, z) \\).\n\n- **Definition**: The partial derivative \\(\\frac{\\partial f}{\\partial x}\\) is the rate of change of \\( f \\) with respect to \\( x \\) while holding all other variables constant.\n- **Example**: For \\( f(x, y) = x^2y + 3xy^2 \\),\n  \\[\n  \\frac{\\partial f}{\\partial x} = 2xy + 3y^2\n  \\]\n  \\[\n  \\frac{\\partial f}{\\partial y} = x^2 + 6xy\n  \\]\n\n### Gradient \\(\\nabla f\\)\n\nThe symbol \\(\\nabla f\\) denotes the gradient of a function \\( f \\). The gradient is a vector-valued function that represents the direction and rate of the steepest increase of \\( f \\).\n\n- **Definition**: The gradient \\(\\nabla f\\) is a\n========== daily/d07_beam10m_scale_group_03_1m/d07_beam10m_conv_04_plan-1.md:35371-35695 [score=10.6011] ==========\n\\partial y} (x^3 y + y^3) = x^3 + 3y^2\n  \\]\n\n- **Gradient Vector**:\n  \\[\n  \\nabla g = (3x^2 y, x^3 + 3y^2)\n  \\]\n\nBy working through these examples, you'll gain a deeper understanding of the gradient and its role in vector calculus.\n\n## User\n\nI need help with a problem from my calculus practice, where I worked on computing the gradient of f=x⁴+y². Now, I want to apply the same concept to a different problem, but I'm stuck on how to find the gradient of f=x³y². I've tried using the product rule, but I'm not getting the right answer. Can you help me understand where I'm going wrong and provide a clear explanation of the concept of gradients in vector calculus? ->-> 6,3\n\n## Assistant\n\nCertainly! Let's walk through the process of finding the gradient of the function \\( f(x, y) = x^3 y^2 \\) and clarify the concept of gradients in vector calculus.\n\n### Definition of the Gradient\n\nThe gradient of a scalar function \\( f(x, y) \\) is a vector field denoted by \\( \\nabla f \\). It is defined as the vector whose components are the partial derivatives of \\( f \\) with respect to each variable. Mathematically, it is given by:\n\n\\[\n\\nabla f = \\left( \\frac{\\partial f}{\\partial x}, \\frac{\\partial f}{\\partial y} \\right)\n\\]\n\n### Steps to Compute the Gradient\n\nFor the function \\( f(x, y) = x^3 y^2 \\):\n\n1. **Compute the Partial Derivative with Respect to \\( x \\)**:\n   - Treat \\( y \\) as a constant and differentiate \\( f \\) with respect to \\( x \\):\n     \\[\n     \\frac{\\partial f}{\\partial x} = \\frac{\\partial}{\\partial x} (x^3 y^2) = 3x^2 y^2\n     \\]\n\n2. **Compute the Partial Derivative with Respect to \\( y \\)**:\n   - Treat \\( x \\) as a constant and differentiate \\( f \\) with respect to \\( y \\):\n     \\[\n     \\frac{\\partial f}{\\partial y} = \\frac{\\partial}{\\partial y} (x^3 y^2) = 2x^3 y\n     \\]\n\n3. **Form the Gradient Vector**:\n   - Combine the partial derivatives to form the gradient vector:\n     \\[\n     \\nabla f = \\left( \\frac{\\partial f}{\\partial x}, \\frac{\\partial f}{\\partial y} \\right) = (3x^2 y^2, 2x^3 y)\n     \\]\n\n### Intuition Behind the Gradient\n\nThe gradient vector \\( \\nabla f \\) at a point \\((x, y)\\) provides important information about the function \\( f \\):\n\n1. **Direction of Steepest Ascent**:\n   - The gradient vector points in the direction of the greatest rate of increase of the function \\( f \\). If you imagine standing at a point \\((x, y)\\) on a landscape described by \\( f \\), the gradient vector tells you which way to go to climb the steepest path.\n\n2. **Magnitude of Change**:\n   - The magnitude of the gradient vector \\( |\\nabla f| \\) indicates the rate of change of \\( f \\) in the direction of the gradient. If the magnitude is large, the function is changing rapidly; if it is small, the function is changing slowly.\n\n3. **Orthogonality to Level Curves**:\n   - The gradient vector is orthogonal (perpendicular) to the level curves of the function \\( f \\). A level curve is a curve along which the value of \\( f \\) is constant. For example, for \\( f(x, y) = x^3 y^2 \\), the level curves are given by \\( x^3 y^2 = c \\) for some constant \\( c \\).\n\n### Example Verification\n\nLet's verify this with a specific point, say \\((1, 1)\\):\n\n- At \\((1, 1)\\):\n  \\[\n  \\nabla f(1, 1) = (3 \\cdot 1^2 \\cdot 1^2, 2 \\cdot 1^3 \\cdot 1) = (3, 2)\n  \\]\n\nThis means at the point \\((1, 1)\\), the function \\( f(x, y) = x^3 y^2 \\) increases most rapidly in the direction of the vector \\((3, 2)\\).\n\n### Summary\n\nThe gradient of \\( f(x, y) = x^3 y^2 \\) is:\n\n\\[\n\\nabla f = (3x^2 y^2, 2x^3 y)\n\\]\n\n### Further Practice\n\nTo solidify your understanding, try computing the gradient for other functions and visualize the results. Let's go through the notation and computation for the curl of a vector field \\( \\mathbf{F} \\). Specifically, we will compute the curl of \\( \\mathbf{F} = (x, z, y) \\).\n\n### Notation Standards for Vector Operations\n\nThe curl of a vector field \\( \\mathbf{F} \\) is denoted by \\( \\nabla \\times \\mathbf{F} \\), where \\( \\nabla \\) (nabla) is the del operator. The curl is a vector operation that describes the infinitesimal rotation of a vector field.\n\n### Del Operator and Curl\n\nThe del operator \\( \\nabla \\) in three dimensions is:\n\\[\n\\nabla = \\left( \\frac{\\partial}{\\partial x}, \\frac{\\partial}{\\partial y}, \\frac{\\partial}{\\partial z} \\right)\n\\]\n\nThe curl of a vector field \\( \\mathbf{F} = (F_1, F_2, F_3) \\) is given by the cross product of \\( \\nabla \\) and \\( \\mathbf{F} \\):\n\\[\n\\nabla \\times \\mathbf{F} = \\left| \\begin{matrix}\n\\mathbf{i} & \\mathbf{j} & \\mathbf{k} \\\\\n\\frac{\\partial}{\\partial x} & \\frac{\\partial}{\\partial y} & \\frac{\\partial}{\\partial z} \\\\\nF_1 & F_2 & F_3\n\\end{matrix} \\right|\n\\]\n\nWhere \\( \\mathbf{i} \\), \\( \\mathbf{j} \\), and \\( \\mathbf{k} \\) are the unit vectors in the \\( x \\), \\( y \\), and \\( z \\) directions, respectively.\n\n### Applying the Notation\n\nGiven \\( \\mathbf{F} = (x, z, y) \\), we have:\n\\[\nF_1 = x, \\quad F_2 = z, \\quad F_3 = y\n\\]\n\nNow, compute the curl using the determinant of the matrix:\n\\[\n\\nabla \\times \\mathbf{F} = \\left| \\begin{matrix}\n\\mathbf{i} & \\mathbf{j} & \\mathbf{k} \\\\\n\\frac{\\partial}{\\partial x} & \\frac{\\partial}{\\partial y} & \\frac{\\partial}{\\partial z} \\\\\nx & z & y\n\\end{matrix} \\right|\n\\]\n\nExpanding this determinant, we get:\n\\[\n\\nabla \\times \\mathbf{F} = \\mathbf{i} \\left( \\frac{\\partial y}{\\partial y} - \\frac{\\partial z}{\\partial z} \\right) - \\mathbf{j} \\left( \\frac{\\partial y}{\\partial x} - \\frac{\\partial x}{\\partial z} \\right) + \\mathbf{k} \\left( \\frac{\\partial z}{\\partial x} - \\frac{\\partial x}{\\partial y} \\right)\n\\]\n\nSimplify each term:\n\\[\n\\frac{\\partial y}{\\partial y} = 1, \\quad \\frac{\\partial z}{\\partial z} = 1, \\quad \\frac{\\partial y}{\\partial x} = 0, \\quad \\frac{\\partial x}{\\partial z} = 0, \\quad \\frac{\\partial z}{\\partial x} = 0, \\quad \\frac{\\partial x}{\\partial y} = 0\n\\]\n\nSo, the curl becomes:\n\\[\n\\nabla \\times \\mathbf{F} = \\mathbf{i} (1 - 1) - \\mathbf{j} (0 - 0) + \\mathbf{k} (0 - 0) = \\mathbf{i} \\cdot 0 - \\mathbf{j} \\cdot 0 + \\mathbf{k} \\cdot 0 = \\mathbf{0}\n\\]\n\n### Final Result\n\nThe curl of \\( \\mathbf{F} = (x, z, y) \\) is:\n\\[\n\\nabla \\times \\mathbf{F} = \\mathbf{0}\n\\]\n\n### Summary\n\nTo write the curl of \\( \\mathbf{F} = (x, z, y) \\) correctly, you use the notation \\( \\nabla \\times \\mathbf{F} \\). The computation involves taking the cross product of the del operator with the vector field, which results in:\n\\[\n\\nabla \\times \\mathbf{F} = \\mathbf{0}\n\\]\n\nIf you have any more questions or need further clarification, feel free to ask!\n\n## User\n\nCan you help me set up a plan to improve my ODE accuracy from 80% to 90% by solving 30 problems from Section 2.1 over the next few days, I need to make sure I'm on track to meet my goal ->-> 5,5\n\n## Assistant\n\nAbsolutely! Finally, we'll discuss how this knowledge can help you achieve your goal of mastering partial derivatives.\n\n### Concept of Optimization and Gradients\n\nThe gradient of a function \\( f(x, y) \\), denoted by \\( \\nabla f \\), is a vector that points in the direction of the steepest ascent of the function. This is because the gradient is defined as:\n\n\\[\n\\nabla f = \\left( \\frac{\\partial f}{\\partial x}, \\frac{\\partial f}{\\partial y} \\right)\n\\]\n\nThe magnitude of the gradient vector represents the rate of change of the function in the direction of the gradient. Therefore, the direction of the gradient vector is the direction in which the function increases most rapidly.\n\n### Example: \\( f(x, y) = 5 - x^2 - y^2 \\)\n\nLet's consider the function \\( f(x, y) = 5 - x^2 - y^2 \\).\n\n1. **Compute the partial derivatives:**\n\n   - Partial derivative with respect to \\( x \\):\n     \\[\n     \\frac{\\partial f}{\\partial x} = -2x\n     \\]\n\n   - Partial derivative with respect to \\( y \\):\n     \\[\n     \\frac{\\partial f}{\\partial y} = -2y\n     \\]\n\n2. **Form the gradient vector:**\n\n   \\[\n   \\nabla f = (-2x, -2y)\n   \\]\n\n3. **Find the critical points:**\n\n   Set the gradient vector to zero to find the critical points:\n   \\[\n   \\nabla f = (-2x, -2y) = (0, 0)\n   \\]\n\n   This gives us the system of equations:\n   \\[\n   -2x = 0 \\implies x = 0\n   \\]\n   \\[\n   -2y = 0 \\implies y = 0\n   \\]\n\n   So, the critical point is \\( (0, 0) \\).\n\n4. **Evaluate the function at the critical point:**\n\n   \\[\n   f(0, 0) = 5 - 0^2 - 0^2 = 5\n   \\]\n\n5. **Determine the nature of the critical point:**\n\n   To determine whether this critical point is a maximum, minimum, or saddle point, we can use the second partial derivatives and the Hessian matrix. The Hessian matrix \\( H \\) is given by:\n   \\[\n   H = \\begin{pmatrix}\n   \\frac{\\partial^2 f}{\\partial x^2} & \\frac{\\partial^2 f}{\\partial x \\partial y} \\\\\n   \\frac{\\partial^2 f}{\\partial y \\partial x} & \\frac{\\partial^2 f}{\\partial y^2}\n   \\end{pmatrix}\n   \\]\n\n   Compute the second partial derivatives:\n   \\[\n   \\frac{\\partial^2 f}{\\partial x^2} = -2\n   \\]\n   \\[\n   \\frac{\\partial^2 f}{\\partial y^2} = -2\n   \\]\n   \\[\n   \\frac{\\partial^2 f}{\\partial x \\partial y} = 0\n   \\]\n   \\[\n   \\frac{\\partial^2 f}{\\partial y \\partial x} = 0\n   \\]\n\n   So, the Hessian matrix is:\n   \\[\n   H = \\begin{pmatrix}\n   -2 & 0 \\\\\n   0 & -2\n   \\end{pmatrix}\n   \\]\n\n   The determinant of the Hessian matrix is:\n   \\[\n   \\det(H) = (-2)(-2) - (0)(0) = 4\n   \\]\n\n   Since the determinant is positive and the leading principal minor (the top-left element) is negative, the Hessian is negative definite. This indicates that the critical point \\( (0, 0) \\) is a local maximum.\n\n### Application to Calculus and Analysis\n\nUnderstanding gradients and optimization is crucial for solving problems in calculus and analysis. Let's re-examine the method.\n\n### Efficient Method: Substitution\n\nAn efficient method to solve this ODE is to use the substitution \\( v = \\frac{y}{x} \\). Then \\( y = vx \\) and \\( \\frac{dy}{dx} = v + x\\frac{dv}{dx} \\).\n\n1. **Substitute \\( y = vx \\) and \\( \\frac{dy}{dx} = v + x\\frac{dv}{dx} \\) into the ODE:**\n\n   \\[\n   v + x\\frac{dv}{dx} = \\frac{x + 2vx}{x} = 1 + 2v\n   \\]\n\n2. **Isolate \\( x\\frac{dv}{dx} \\):**\n\n   \\[\n   x\\frac{dv}{dx} = 1 + 2v - v = 1 + v\n   \\]\n\n3. **Separate variables and integrate:**\n\n   \\[\n   \\frac{dv}{1 + v} = \\frac{dx}{x}\n   \\]\n\n   Integrate both sides:\n   \\[\n   \\int \\frac{dv}{1 + v} = \\int \\frac{dx}{x}\n   \\]\n\n   \\[\n   \\ln|1 + v| = \\ln|x| + C\n   \\]\n\n   Exponentiate both sides:\n   \\[\n   |1 + v| = Cx\n   \\]\n\n   Remove the absolute value:\n   \\[\n   1 + v = Cx \\quad \\text{or} \\quad 1 + v = -Cx\n   \\]\n\n   Since \\( v = \\frac{y}{x} \\):\n   \\[\n   1 + \\frac{y}{x} = Cx \\quad \\text{or} \\quad 1 + \\frac{y}{x} = -Cx\n   \\]\n\n   Multiply through by \\( x \\):\n   \\[\n   x + y = Cx^2 \\quad \\text{or} \\quad x + y = -Cx^2\n   \\]\n\n   Rearrange to solve for \\( y \\):\n   \\[\n   y = Cx^2 - x \\quad \\text{or} \\quad y = -Cx^2 - x\n   \\]\n\n   Combining these, we get:\n   \\[\n   y = Cx^2 - x\n   \\]\n\n### Conclusion\n\nYour initial solution \\( y = 0.5x + Cx \\) is not correct. The correct general solution is:\n\\[\n\\boxed{y = Cx^2 - x}\n\\]\n\n### Efficiency\n\nUsing the substitution \\( v = \\frac{y}{x} \\) is generally more efficient and leads directly to the correct solution. Understanding the continuity of functions is indeed crucial for Partial Differential Equations (PDEs). Let's break down why the function \\( f(x, y) = \\sin(xy) \\) is continuous everywhere.\n\n### Continuity of Functions\n\nA function \\( f(x, y) \\) is continuous at a point \\((a, b)\\) if:\n\n1. \\( f(a, b) \\) exists.\n2. \\(\\lim_{(x, y) \\to (a, b)} f(x, y)\\) exists.\n3. \\(\\lim_{(x, y) \\to (a, b)} f(x, y) = f(a, b)\\).\n\n### Analyzing \\( f(x, y) = \\sin(xy) \\)\n\n1. **Existence of \\( f(x, y) \\):**\n   - The function \\( \\sin(xy) \\) is defined for all real numbers \\( x \\) and \\( y \\). Therefore, \\( f(x, y) \\) exists for all \\((x, y) \\in \\mathbb{R}^2\\).\n\n2. **Existence of the Limit:**\n   - The sine function, \\( \\sin(z) \\), is continuous for all real numbers \\( z \\). This means that for any sequence \\((x_n, y_n)\\) approaching \\((a, b)\\), the limit \\(\\lim_{n \\to \\infty} \\sin(x_n y_n)\\) exists and equals \\(\\sin(ab)\\).\n\n3. **Equality of the Limit and the Function Value:**\n   - Since \\( \\sin(z) \\) is continuous, we have:\n     \\[\n     \\lim_{(x, y) \\to (a, b)} \\sin(xy) = \\sin\\left(\\lim_{(x, y) \\to (a, b)} xy\\right) = \\sin(ab).\n     \\]\n   - Therefore, \\(\\lim_{(x, y) \\to (a, b)} \\sin(xy) = \\sin(ab) = f(a, b)\\).\n\n### Conclusion\n\nSince \\( f(x, y) = \\sin(xy) \\) satisfies all three conditions of continuity at every point \\((a, b) \\in \\mathbb{R}^2\\), we conclude that \\( f(x, y) = \\sin(xy) \\) is continuous everywhere.\n\n### Summary\n\n- **Existence:** \\( \\sin(xy) \\) is defined for all \\( (x, y) \\).\n- **Limit Existence:** The limit \\(\\lim_{(x, y) \\to (a, b)} \\sin(xy)\\) exists and equals \\(\\sin(ab)\\).\n- **Equality:** The limit equals the function value at \\((a, b)\\).\n\nThus, \\( f(x, y) = \\sin(xy) \\) is continuous everywhere.\n\n### Further Practice\n\nTo solidify your understanding of continuity in multivariable functions, consider the following steps:\n- **Practice More Problems:** Solve additional problems involving continuity of functions.\n- **Review Definitions:** Ensure you understand the formal definition of continuity and how it applies to multivariable functions.\n- **Visualize Functions:** Use graphing tools to visualize functions and their behavior around different points.\n\nBy consistently practicing and reviewing these concepts, you will build a strong foundation in understanding continuity, which is essential for PDEs. For vectors \\(\\mathbf{v}_1, \\mathbf{v}_2, \\ldots, \\mathbf{v}_n\\), the span is given by:\n\\[\n\\text{Span}(\\mathbf{v}_1, \\mathbf{v}_2, \\ldots, \\mathbf{v}_n) = \\left\\{ c_1 \\mathbf{v}_1 + c_2 \\mathbf{v}_2 + \\cdots + c_n \\mathbf{v}_n \\mid c_1, c_2, \\ldots, c_n \\in \\mathbb{R} \\right\\}\n\\]\n\n### Step 2: Applying the Concept to Your Vectors\nFor the vectors \\(\\mathbf{v}_1 = [1, 0]\\) and \\(\\mathbf{v}_2 = [0, 1]\\), the span is:\n\\[\n\\text{Span}([1, 0], [0, 1]) = \\left\\{ c_1 [1, 0] + c_2 [0, 1] \\mid c_1, c_2 \\in \\mathbb{R} \\right\\}\n\\]\n\n### Step 3: Expressing Any Vector in \\(\\mathbb{R}^2\\)\nAny vector \\(\\mathbf{v} = [a, b]\\) in \\(\\mathbb{R}^2\\) can be written as:\n\\[\n[a, b] = a[1, 0] + b[0, 1]\n\\]\n\nThis shows that any vector in \\(\\mathbb{R}^2\\) can be expressed as a linear combination of \\([1, 0]\\) and \\([0, 1]\\). Let's start with a basic explanation of integration and then discuss how it fits into your broader study goals for PDEs and ODEs.\n\n### What is Integration?\n\nIntegration is a fundamental concept in calculus that deals with the accumulation of quantities. It is essentially the reverse process of differentiation and can be thought of as finding the area under a curve or the total accumulation of a quantity over a range.\n\nThere are two main types of integrals:\n\n1. **Indefinite Integral**: This is the antiderivative of a function. It represents a family of functions whose derivatives are the original function. It is written as:\n   \\[\n   \\int f(x) \\, dx = F(x) + C\n   \\]\n   where \\( F(x) \\) is an antiderivative of \\( f(x) \\) and \\( C \\) is the constant of integration.\n\n2. **Definite Integral**: This calculates the net area between the function and the x-axis over a specified interval \\([a, b]\\). It is written as:\n   \\[\n   \\int_a^b f(x) \\, dx\n   \\]\n   and it yields a specific number.\n\n### Basic Properties of Integration\n\n- **Linearity**: The integral of a sum of functions is the sum of the integrals of the functions.\n  \\[\n  \\int (f(x) + g(x)) \\, dx = \\int f(x) \\, dx + \\int g(x) \\, dx\n  \\]\n\n- **Constant Multiple Rule**: A constant factor can be moved outside the integral.\n  \\[\n  \\int k f(x) \\, dx = k \\int f(x) \\, dx\n  \\]\n\n### Techniques of Integration\n\n- **Substitution (u-substitution)**: This method is used to simplify the integrand by changing variables.\n  \\[\n  \\int f(g(x)) g'(x) \\, dx = \\int f(u) \\, du \\quad \\text{where} \\quad u = g(x)\n  \\]\n\n- **Integration by Parts**: This method is derived from the product rule of differentiation.\n  \\[\n  \\int u \\, dv = uv - \\int v \\, du\n  \\]\n\n- **Partial Fractions**: This technique is used to decompose rational functions into simpler fractions that can be integrated easily.\n\n### Application in PDEs and ODEs\n\nUnderstanding integration is crucial for solving differential equations, whether they are ordinary (ODEs) or partial (PDEs). Here are a few ways integration is used:\n\n- **Solving ODEs**: Many ODEs can be solved by integrating both sides of the equation. For example, the first-order linear ODE \\( \\frac{dy}{dx} + P(x)y = Q(x) \\) can be solved using an integrating factor.\n\n- **Solving PDEs**: Integration plays a key role in solving PDEs, particularly in methods like separation of variables, where you often need to integrate to find the solution.\n\n### Setting Learning Goals Based on Assessments\n\nGiven your goal to achieve 92% accuracy on 30 ODE problems from Chapter 2, here are some steps to help you reach that goal:\n\n1. **Identify Weak Areas**: Review the problems you got wrong and understand why. The characteristic equation is given by:\n\n\\[\n\\det(A - \\lambda I) = 0\n\\]\n\nwhere \\( I \\) is the identity matrix of the same size as \\( A \\).\n\nFor our matrix \\( A \\):\n\n\\[\nA - \\lambda I = \\begin{bmatrix} 1 & 2 \\\\ 2 & 1 \\end{bmatrix} - \\lambda \\begin{bmatrix} 1 & 0 \\\\  0 & 1 \\end{bmatrix} = \\begin{bmatrix} 1 - \\lambda & 2 \\\\ 2 & 1 - \\lambda \\end{bmatrix}\n\\]\n\nNext, we need to find the determinant of this matrix:\n\n\\[\n\\det(A - \\lambda I) = \\det \\begin{bmatrix} 1 - \\lambda & 2 \\\\ 2 & 1 - \\lambda \\end{bmatrix}\n\\]\n\nThe determinant of a 2x2 matrix \\(\\begin{bmatrix} a & b \\\\ c & d \\end{bmatrix}\\) is calculated as \\( ad - bc \\). Can you help me understand where I'm going wrong and provide a clear explanation of the concept of gradients in vector calculus? ->-> 6,3\n\n## Assistant\n\nCertainly! Let's walk through the process of finding the gradient of the function \\( f(x, y) = x^3 y^2 \\) and clarify the concept of gradients in vector calculus.\n\n### Definition of the Gradient\n\nThe gradient of a scalar function \\( f(x, y) \\) is a vector field denoted by \\( \\nabla f \\). It is defined as the vector whose components are the partial derivatives of \\( f \\) with respect to each variable. Mathematically, it is given by:\n\n\\[\n\\nabla f = \\left( \\frac{\\partial f}{\\partial x}, \\frac{\\partial f}{\\partial y} \\right)\n\\]\n\n### Steps to Compute the Gradient\n\nFor the function \\( f(x, y) = x^3 y^2 \\):\n\n1. **Compute the Partial Derivative with Respect to \\( x \\)**:\n   - Treat \\( y \\) as a constant and differentiate \\( f \\) with respect to \\( x \\):\n     \\[\n     \\frac{\\partial f}{\\partial x} = \\frac{\\partial}{\\partial x} (x^3 y^2) = 3x^2 y^2\n     \\]\n\n2. **Compute the Partial Derivative with Respect to \\( y \\)**:\n   - Treat \\( x \\) as a constant and differentiate \\( f \\) with respect to \\( y \\):\n     \\[\n     \\frac{\\partial f}{\\partial y} = \\frac{\\partial}{\\partial y} (x^3 y^2) = 2x^3 y\n     \\]\n\n3. **Form the Gradient Vector**:\n   - Combine the partial derivatives to form the gradient vector:\n     \\[\n     \\nabla f = \\left( \\frac{\\partial f}{\\partial x}, \\frac{\\partial f}{\\partial y} \\right) = (3x^2 y^2, 2x^3 y)\n     \\]\n\n### Intuition Behind the Gradient\n\nThe gradient vector \\( \\nabla f \\) at a point \\((x, y)\\) provides important information about the function \\( f \\):\n\n1. **Direction of Steepest Ascent**:\n   - The gradient vector points in the direction of the greatest rate of increase of the function \\( f \\). If you imagine standing at a point \\((x, y)\\) on a landscape described by \\( f \\), the gradient vector tells you which way to go to climb the steepest path.\n\n2. **Magnitude of Change**:\n   - The magnitude of the gradient vector \\( |\\nabla f| \\) indicates the rate of change of \\( f \\) in the direction of the gradient. If the magnitude is large, the function is changing rapidly; if it is small, the function is changing slowly.\n\n3. **Orthogonality to Level Curves**:\n   - The gradient vector is orthogonal (perpendicular) to the level curves of the function \\( f \\). A level curve is a curve along which the value of \\( f \\) is constant. For example, for \\( f(x, y) = x^3 y^2 \\), the level curves are given by \\( x^3 y^2 = c \\) for some constant \\( c \\).\n\n### Example Verification\n\nLet's verify this with a specific point, say \\((1, 1)\\):\n\n- At \\((1, 1)\\):\n  \\[\n  \\nabla f(1, 1) = (3 \\cdot 1^2 \\cdot 1^2, 2 \\cdot 1^3 \\cdot 1) = (3, 2)\n  \\]\n\nThis means at the point \\((1, 1)\\), the function \\( f(x, y) = x^3 y^2 \\) increases most rapidly in the direction of the vector \\((3, 2)\\).\n\n### Summary\n\nThe gradient of \\( f(x, y) = x^3 y^2 \\) is:\n\n\\[\n\\nabla f = (3x^2 y^2, 2x^3 y)\n\\]\n\n### Further Practice\n\nTo solidify your understanding, try computing the gradient for other functions and visualize the results. For example, consider the function \\( g(x, y) = x^2 y^3 + x y^2 \\) and compute its gradient:\n\n\\[\n\\nabla g = \\left( \\frac{\\partial g}{\\partial x}, \\frac{\\partial g}{\\partial y} \\right)\n\\]\n\n- **Partial Derivative with Respect to \\( x \\)**:\n  \\[\n  \\frac{\\partial g}{\\partial x} = \\frac{\\partial}{\\partial x} (x^2 y^3 + x y^2) = 2x y^3 + y^2\n  \\]\n\n- **Partial Derivative with Respect to \\( y \\)**:\n  \\[\n  \\frac{\\partial g}{\\partial y} = \\frac{\\partial}{\\partial y} (x^2 y^3 + x y^2) = 3x^2 y^2 + 2x y\n  \\]\n\n- **Gradient Vector**:\n  \\[\n  \\nabla g = (2x y^3 + y^2, 3x^2 y^2 + 2x y)\n  \\]\n\nBy working through these examples, you'll gain a deeper understanding of the gradient and its role in vector calculus.\n\n## User\n\nI'm trying to solve the linear system x+3y+z=6, 2x-y+z=3, x+y-z=0, and I found that x=1, y=2, z=1, but I'm not sure if I did it correctly, can you verify my solution? Specifically, we will compute the curl of \\( \\mathbf{F} = (x, z, y) \\).\n\n### Notation Standards for Vector Operations\n\nThe curl of a vector field \\( \\mathbf{F} \\) is denoted by \\( \\nabla \\times \\mathbf{F} \\), where \\( \\nabla \\) (nabla) is the del operator. The curl is a vector operation that describes the infinitesimal rotation of a vector field.\n\n### Del Operator and Curl\n\nThe del operator \\( \\nabla \\) in three dimensions is:\n\\[\n\\nabla = \\left( \\frac{\\partial}{\\partial x}, \\frac{\\partial}{\\partial y}, \\frac{\\partial}{\\partial z} \\right)\n\\]\n\nThe curl of a vector field \\( \\mathbf{F} = (F_1, F_2, F_3) \\) is given by the cross product of \\( \\nabla \\) and \\( \\mathbf{F} \\):\n\\[\n\\nabla \\times \\mathbf{F} = \\left| \\begin{matrix}\n\\mathbf{i} & \\mathbf{j} & \\mathbf{k} \\\\\n\\frac{\\partial}{\\partial x} & \\frac{\\partial}{\\partial y} & \\frac{\\partial}{\\partial z} \\\\\nF_1 & F_2 & F_3\n\\end{matrix} \\right|\n\\]\n\nWhere \\( \\mathbf{i} \\), \\( \\mathbf{j} \\), and \\( \\mathbf{k} \\) are the unit vectors in the \\( x \\), \\( y \\), and \\( z \\) directions, respectively.\n\n### Applying the Notation\n\nGiven \\( \\mathbf{F} = (x, z, y) \\), we have:\n\\[\nF_1 = x, \\quad F_2 = z, \\quad F_3 = y\n\\]\n\nNow, compute the curl using the determinant of the matrix:\n\\[\n\\nabla \\times \\mathbf{F} = \\left| \\begin{matrix}\n\\mathbf{i} & \\mathbf{j} & \\mathbf{k} \\\\\n\\frac{\\partial}{\\partial x} & \\frac{\\partial}{\\partial y} & \\frac{\\partial}{\\partial z} \\\\\nx & z & y\n\\end{matrix} \\right|\n\\]\n\nExpanding this determinant, we get:\n\\[\n\\nabla \\times \\mathbf{F} = \\mathbf{i} \\left( \\frac{\\partial y}{\\partial y} - \\frac{\\partial z}{\\partial z} \\right) - \\mathbf{j} \\left( \\frac{\\partial y}{\\partial x} - \\frac{\\partial x}{\\partial z} \\right) + \\mathbf{k} \\left( \\frac{\\partial z}{\\partial x} - \\frac{\\partial x}{\\partial y} \\right)\n\\]\n\nSimplify each term:\n\\[\n\\frac{\\partial y}{\\partial y} = 1, \\quad \\frac{\\partial z}{\\partial z} = 1, \\quad \\frac{\\partial y}{\\partial x} = 0, \\quad \\frac{\\partial x}{\\partial z} = 0, \\quad \\frac{\\partial z}{\\partial x} = 0, \\quad \\frac{\\partial x}{\\partial y} = 0\n\\]\n\nSo, the curl becomes:\n\\[\n\\nabla \\times \\mathbf{F} = \\mathbf{i} (1 - 1) - \\mathbf{j} (0 - 0) + \\mathbf{k} (0 - 0) = \\mathbf{i} \\cdot 0 - \\mathbf{j} \\cdot 0 + \\mathbf{k} \\cdot 0 = \\mathbf{0}\n\\]\n\n### Final Result\n\nThe curl of \\( \\mathbf{F} = (x, z, y) \\) is:\n\\[\n\\nabla \\times \\mathbf{F} = \\mathbf{0}\n\\]\n\n### Summary\n\nTo write the curl of \\( \\mathbf{F} = (x, z, y) \\) correctly, you use the notation \\( \\nabla \\times \\mathbf{F} \\). the Integrating Factor\nThe standard form of a first-order linear differential equation is:\n\\[\n\\frac{dy}{dx} + P(x)y = Q(x)\n\\]\n\nHere, \\( P(x) = 3 \\) and \\( Q(x) = 6 \\).\n\nThe integrating User\n\nI'm trying to understand the concept of optimization and how it relates to gradients - can you explain why ∇f points to the steepest ascent for a function f(x,y), and provide an example, such as f(x,y) = 5 - x^2 - y^2, and how this concept can be applied to solve problems in calculus and analysis, and help me achieve my goal of mastering partial derivatives by solving 20 problems from Section 14.1 with at least 90% accuracy? ->-> 2,18\n\n## Assistant\n\nCertainly! Let's delve into the concept of optimization and how it relates to gradients, and then provide an example to illustrate these ideas. Finally, we'll discuss how this knowledge can help you achieve your goal of mastering partial derivatives.\n\n### Concept of Optimization and Gradients\n\nThe gradient of a function \\( f(x, y) \\), denoted by \\( \\nabla f \\), is a vector that points in the direction of the steepest ascent of the function. This is because the gradient is defined as:\n\n\\[\n\\nabla f = \\left( \\frac{\\partial f}{\\partial x}, \\frac{\\partial f}{\\partial y} \\right)\n\\]\n\nThe magnitude of the gradient vector represents the rate of change of the function in the direction of the gradient. Therefore, the direction of the gradient vector is the direction in which the function increases most rapidly.\n\n### Example: \\( f(x, y) = 5 - x^2 - y^2 \\)\n\nLet's consider the function \\( f(x, y) = 5 - x^2 - y^2 \\).\n\n1. **Compute the partial derivatives:**\n\n   - Partial derivative with respect to \\( x \\):\n     \\[\n     \\frac{\\partial f}{\\partial x} = -2x\n     \\]\n\n   - Partial derivative with respect to \\( y \\):\n     \\[\n     \\frac{\\partial f}{\\partial y} = -2y\n     \\]\n\n2. **Form the gradient vector:**\n\n   \\[\n   \\nabla f = (-2x, -2y)\n   \\]\n\n3. **Find the critical points:**\n\n   Set the gradient vector to zero to find the critical points:\n   \\[\n   \\nabla f = (-2x, -2y) = (0, 0)\n   \\]\n\n   This gives us the system of equations:\n   \\[\n   -2x = 0 \\implies x = 0\n   \\]\n   \\[\n   -2y = 0 \\implies y = 0\n   \\]\n\n   So, the critical point is \\( (0, 0) \\).\n\n4. **Evaluate the function at the critical point:**\n\n   \\[\n   f(0, 0) = 5 - 0^2 - 0^2 = 5\n   \\]\n\n5. **Determine the nature of the critical point:**\n\n   To determine whether this critical point is a maximum, minimum, or saddle point, we can use the second partial derivatives and the Hessian matrix. The Hessian matrix \\( H \\) is given by:\n   \\[\n   H = \\begin{pmatrix}\n   \\frac{\\partial^2 f}{\\partial x^2} & \\frac{\\partial^2 f}{\\partial x \\partial y} \\\\\n   \\frac{\\partial^2 f}{\\partial y \\partial x} & \\frac{\\partial^2 f}{\\partial y^2}\n   \\end{pmatrix}\n   \\]\n\n   Compute the second partial derivatives:\n   \\[\n   \\frac{\\partial^2 f}{\\partial x^2} = -2\n   \\]\n   \\[\n   \\frac{\\partial^2 f}{\\partial y^2} = -2\n   \\]\n   \\[\n   \\frac{\\partial^2 f}{\\partial x \\partial y} = 0\n   \\]\n   \\[\n   \\frac{\\partial^2 f}{\\partial y \\partial x} = 0\n   \\]\n\n   So, the Hessian matrix is:\n   \\[\n   H = \\begin{pmatrix}\n   -2 & 0 \\\\\n   0 & -2\n   \\end{pmatrix}\n   \\]\n\n   The determinant of the Hessian matrix is:\n   \\[\n   \\det(H) = (-2)(-2) - (0)(0) = 4\n   \\]\n\n   Since the determinant is positive and the leading principal minor (the top-left element) is negative, the Hessian is negative definite. This indicates that the critical point \\( (0, 0) \\) is a local maximum.\n\n### Application to Calculus and Analysis\n\nUnderstanding gradients and optimization is crucial for solving problems in calculus and analysis. Let's delve into the concept of gradients and how they relate to optimization, specifically using the function \\( f(x, y) = 5 - x^2 - y^2 \\).\n\n### Concept of Gradients\n\nThe gradient of a function \\( f(x, y) \\) is a vector that points in the direction of the greatest rate of increase of the function. It is denoted by \\( \\nabla f \\) and is defined as:\n\n\\[\n\\nabla f = \\left( \\frac{\\partial f}{\\partial x}, \\frac{\\partial f}{\\partial y} \\right)\n\\]\n\nFor the function \\( f(x, y) = 5 - x^2 - y^2 \\):\n\n1. **Compute the Partial Derivatives:**\n   \\[\n   \\frac{\\partial f}{\\partial x} = -2x\n   \\]\n   \\[\n   \\frac{\\partial f}{\\partial y} = -2y\n   \\]\n\n2. **Form the Gradient Vector:**\n   \\[\n   \\nabla f = (-2x, -2y)\n   \\]\n\n### Visualization of Gradients\n\nTo visualize how \\( \\nabla f \\) points to the steepest ascent, let's consider the function \\( f(x, y) = 5 - x^2 - y^2 \\):\n\n- **Function Shape:**\n  The function \\( f(x, y) = 5 - x^2 - y^2 \\) describes a paraboloid that opens downward, with its maximum value at the origin \\((0, 0)\\).\n\n- **Gradient at Different Points:**\n  - At the origin \\((0, 0)\\):\n    \\[\n    \\nabla f(0, 0) = (-2 \\cdot 0, -2 \\cdot 0) = (0, 0)\n    \\]\n    The gradient is zero, indicating that this is a critical point (maximum in this case).\n\n  - At a point \\((1, 1)\\):\n    \\[\n    \\nabla f(1, 1) = (-2 \\cdot 1, -2 \\cdot 1) = (-2, -2)\n    \\]\n    The gradient vector \\((-2, -2)\\) points towards the origin, which is the direction of steepest ascent.\n\n  - At a point \\((-1, -1)\\):\n    \\[\n    \\nabla f(-1, -1) = (-2 \\cdot (-1), -2 \\cdot (-1)) = (2, 2)\n    \\]\n    The gradient vector \\((2, 2)\\) also points towards the origin.\n\n### Connecting to Optimization\n\nIn optimization, the gradient is used to find the maximum or minimum of a function. For a function \\( f(x, y) \\):\n\n- **Steepest Ascent:**\n  The gradient \\( \\nabla f \\) at any point \\((x, y)\\) points in the direction of the steepest ascent. Moving in the direction of \\( \\nabla f \\) will increase the value of \\( f \\) most rapidly.\n\n- **Steepest Descent:**\n  Moving i\n========== daily/d07_beam10m_scale_group_03_1m/d07_beam10m_conv_04_plan-1.md:65739-65994 [score=11.7237] ==========\n of Equation:**\n   - Recognize that \\( \\frac{d^2y}{dx^2} - 4y = 0 \\) is a second-order linear homogeneous differential equation with constant coefficients.\n\n2. **Form the Characteristic Equation:**\n   - Write the characteristic equation \\( r^2 - 4 = 0 \\).\n\n3. **Solve the Characteristic Equation:**\n   - Solve \\( r^2 - 4 = 0 \\) to find the roots \\( r = \\pm 2 \\).\n\n4. **Construct the General Solution:**\n   - Use the roots to write the general solution \\( y(x) = Ae^{2x} + Be^{-2x} \\).\n\n5. **Verify the Solution:**\n   - Double-check each step to ensure accuracy.\n\nThe 20-minute estimate includes time for understanding the problem, performing calculations, and verifying the solution. Understanding the continuity of functions is indeed crucial for Partial Differential Equations (PDEs). Let's break down why the function \\( f(x, y) = \\sin(xy) \\) is continuous everywhere.\n\n### Continuity of Functions\n\nA function \\( f(x, y) \\) is continuous at a point \\((a, b)\\) if:\n\n1. \\( f(a, b) \\) exists.\n2. \\(\\lim_{(x, y) \\to (a, b)} f(x, y)\\) exists.\n3. \\(\\lim_{(x, y) \\to (a, b)} f(x, y) = f(a, b)\\).\n\n### Analyzing \\( f(x, y) = \\sin(xy) \\)\n\n1. **Existence of \\( f(x, y) \\):**\n   - The function \\( \\sin(xy) \\) is defined for all real numbers \\( x \\) and \\( y \\). Therefore, \\( f(x, y) \\) exists for all \\((x, y) \\in \\mathbb{R}^2\\).\n\n2. **Existence of the Limit:**\n   - The sine function, \\( \\sin(z) \\), is continuous for all real numbers \\( z \\). This means that for any sequence \\((x_n, y_n)\\) approaching \\((a, b)\\), the limit \\(\\lim_{n \\to \\infty} \\sin(x_n y_n)\\) exists and equals \\(\\sin(ab)\\).\n\n3. **Equality of the Limit and the Function Value:**\n   - Since \\( \\sin(z) \\) is continuous, we have:\n     \\[\n     \\lim_{(x, y) \\to (a, b)} \\sin(xy) = \\sin\\left(\\lim_{(x, y) \\to (a, b)} xy\\right) = \\sin(ab).\n     \\]\n   - Therefore, \\(\\lim_{(x, y) \\to (a, b)} \\sin(xy) = \\sin(ab) = f(a, b)\\).\n\n### Conclusion\n\nSince \\( f(x, y) = \\sin(xy) \\) satisfies all three conditions of continuity at every point \\((a, b) \\in \\mathbb{R}^2\\), we conclude that \\( f(x, y) = \\sin(xy) \\) is continuous everywhere.\n\n### Summary\n\n- **Existence:** \\( \\sin(xy) \\) is defined for all \\( (x, y) \\).\n- **Limit Existence:** The limit \\(\\lim_{(x, y) \\to (a, b)} \\sin(xy)\\) exists and equals \\(\\sin(ab)\\).\n- **Equality:** The limit equals the function value at \\((a, b)\\).\n\nThus, \\( f(x, y) = \\sin(xy) \\) is continuous everywhere.\n\n### Further Practice\n\nTo solidify your understanding of continuity in multivariable functions, consider the following steps:\n- **Practice More Problems:** Solve additional problems involving continuity of functions.\n- **Review Definitions:** Ensure you understand the formal definition of continuity and how it applies to multivariable functions.\n- **Visualize Functions:** Use graphing tools to visualize functions and their behavior around different points.\n\nBy consistently practicing and reviewing these concepts, you will build a strong foundation in understanding continuity, which is essential for PDEs. Let's break down the problem and the concepts involved step by step.\n\n### Problem: Finding the Span of Vectors in \\(\\mathbb{R}^2\\)\n\nYou need to find the span of the vectors \\([1, 0]\\) and \\([0, 1]\\) in \\(\\mathbb{R}^2\\).\n\n### Step 1: Understanding Span\nThe span of a set of vectors is the set of all possible linear combinations of those vectors. For vectors \\(\\mathbf{v}_1, \\mathbf{v}_2, \\ldots, \\mathbf{v}_n\\), the span is given by:\n\\[\n\\text{Span}(\\mathbf{v}_1, \\mathbf{v}_2, \\ldots, \\mathbf{v}_n) = \\left\\{ c_1 \\mathbf{v}_1 + c_2 \\mathbf{v}_2 + \\cdots + c_n \\mathbf{v}_n \\mid c_1, c_2, \\ldots, c_n \\in \\mathbb{R} \\right\\}\n\\]\n\n### Step 2: Applying the Concept to Your Vectors\nFor the vectors \\(\\mathbf{v}_1 = [1, 0]\\) and \\(\\mathbf{v}_2 = [0, 1]\\), the span is:\n\\[\n\\text{Span}([1, 0], [0, 1]) = \\left\\{ c_1 [1, 0] + c_2 [0, 1] \\mid c_1, c_2 \\in \\mathbb{R} \\right\\}\n\\]\n\n### Step 3: Expressing Any Vector in \\(\\mathbb{R}^2\\)\nAny vector \\(\\mathbf{v} = [a, b]\\) in \\(\\mathbb{R}^2\\) can be written as:\n\\[\n[a, b] = a[1, 0] + b[0, 1]\n\\]\n\nThis shows that any vector in \\(\\mathbb{R}^2\\) can be expressed as a linear combination of \\([1, 0]\\) and \\([0, 1]\\). Therefore, the span of \\([1, 0]\\) and \\([0, 1]\\) is the entire \\(\\mathbb{R}^2\\).\n\n### Final Answer\nThe span of the vectors \\([1, 0]\\) and \\([0, 1]\\) in \\(\\mathbb{R}^2\\) is:\n\\[\n\\boxed{\\mathbb{R}^2}\n\\]\n\n### Understanding Orthogonality\nOrthogonality is a property of vectors where the dot product of two vectors is zero. For vectors \\(\\mathbf{u} = [u_1, u_2]\\) and \\(\\mathbf{v} = [v_1, v_2]\\), the dot product is given by:\n\\[\n\\mathbf{u} \\cdot \\mathbf{v} = u_1 v_1 + u_2 v_2\n\\]\n\nFor the vectors \\([1, 1]\\) and \\([1, -1]\\):\n\\[\n[1, 1] \\cdot [1, -1] = 1 \\cdot 1 + 1 \\cdot (-1) = 1 - 1 = 0\n\\]\n\nSince the dot product is zero, the vectors \\([1, 1]\\) and \\([1, -1]\\) are indeed orthogonal.\n\n### Summary\n- The span of \\([1, 0]\\) and \\([0, 1]\\) is \\(\\mathbb{R}^2\\).\n- Vectors \\([1, 1]\\) and \\([1, -1]\\) are orthogonal because their dot product is zero.\n\nIf you have any more questions or need further examples, feel free to ask!\n\n## User\n\nI've been trying to solve this problem, but I'm having trouble, can you check my work? I've been studying for my PDE prep and I want to make sure I understand the basics, especially after practicing 14 integration problems from Section 15.1 and getting 12 correct, and I've been focusing on setting learning goals based on assessments, like achieving 92% accuracy on 30 ODE problems from Chapter 2 ->-> 7,7\n\n## Assistant\n\nCertainly! Let's start with a basic explanation of integration and then discuss how it fits into your broader study goals for PDEs and ODEs.\n\n### What is Integration?\n\nIntegration is a fundamental concept in calculus that deals with the accumulation of quantities. It is essentially the reverse process of differentiation and can be thought of as finding the area under a curve or the total accumulation of a quantity over a range.\n\nThere are two main types of integrals:\n\n1. **Indefinite Integral**: This is the antiderivative of a function. It represents a family of functions whose derivatives are the original function. It is written as:\n   \\[\n   \\int f(x) \\, dx = F(x) + C\n   \\]\n   where \\( F(x) \\) is an antiderivative of \\( f(x) \\) and \\( C \\) is the constant of integration.\n\n2. **Definite Integral**: This calculates the net area between the function and the x-axis over a specified interval \\([a, b]\\). It is written as:\n   \\[\n   \\int_a^b f(x) \\, dx\n   \\]\n   and it yields a specific number.\n\n### Basic Properties of Integration\n\n- **Linearity**: The integral of a sum of functions is the sum of the integrals of the functions.\n  \\[\n  \\int (f(x) + g(x)) \\, dx = \\int f(x) \\, dx + \\int g(x) \\, dx\n  \\]\n\n- **Constant Multiple Rule**: A constant factor can be moved outside the integral.\n  \\[\n  \\int k f(x) \\, dx = k \\int f(x) \\, dx\n  \\]\n\n### Techniques of Integration\n\n- **Substitution (u-substitution)**: This method is used to simplify the integrand by changing variables.\n  \\[\n  \\int f(g(x)) g'(x) \\, dx = \\int f(u) \\, du \\quad \\text{where} \\quad u = g(x)\n  \\]\n\n- **Integration by Parts**: This method is derived from the product rule of differentiation.\n  \\[\n  \\int u \\, dv = uv - \\int v \\, du\n  \\]\n\n- **Partial Fractions**: This technique is used to decompose rational functions into simpler fractions that can be integrated easily.\n\n### Application in PDEs and ODEs\n\nUnderstanding integration is crucial for solving differential equations, whether they are ordinary (ODEs) or partial (PDEs). Here are a few ways integration is used:\n\n- **Solving ODEs**: Many ODEs can be solved by integrating both sides of the equation. For example, the first-order linear ODE \\( \\frac{dy}{dx} + P(x)y = Q(x) \\) can be solved using an integrating factor.\n\n- **Solving PDEs**: Integration plays a key role in solving PDEs, particularly in methods like separation of variables, where you often need to integrate to find the solution.\n\n### Setting Learning Goals Based on Assessments\n\nGiven your goal to achieve 92% accuracy on 30 ODE problems from Chapter 2, here are some steps to help you reach that goal:\n\n1. **Identify Weak Areas**: Review the problems you got wrong and understand why. Are there specific techniques or concepts you're struggling with?\n\n2. **Focused Practice**: Spend extra time practicing those specific areas. For example, if you're having trouble with integration by parts, find more problems that focus on that technique.\n\n3. **Regular Review**: Regularly review the material to reinforce your understanding. This can include reworking problems you previously solved and reviewing key concepts.\n\n4. **Seek Help**: If you're consistently struggling with certain types of problems, consider seeking help from a tutor or discussing the problems with classmates.\n\n5. **Set Milestones**: Break your larger goal into smaller, achievable milestones. For example, aim to solve 10 problems correctly in a week and gradually increase the difficulty.\n\n### Example Problem\n\nLet's solve a simple integration problem to illustrate the process:\n\n**Problem**: Evaluate the definite integral \\(\\int_0^1 3x^2 \\, dx\\).\n\n**Solution**:\n1. Find the antiderivative of \\(3x^2\\):\n   \\[\n   \\int 3x^2 \\, dx = x^3 + C\n   \\]\n\n2. Let's walk through the process of finding the eigenvalues and eigenvectors for the matrix \\( A = \\begin{bmatrix} 1 & 2 \\\\ 2 & 1 \\end{bmatrix} \\).\n\n### Step 1: Find the Eigenvalues\n\nEigenvalues (\\(\\lambda\\)) are found by solving the characteristic equation of the matrix \\( A \\). The characteristic equation is given by:\n\n\\[\n\\det(A - \\lambda I) = 0\n\\]\n\nwhere \\( I \\) is the identity matrix of the same size as \\( A \\).\n\nFor our matrix \\( A \\):\n\n\\[\nA - \\lambda I = \\begin{bmatrix} 1 & 2 \\\\ 2 & 1 \\end{bmatrix} - \\lambda \\begin{bmatrix} 1 & 0 \\\\  0 & 1 \\end{bmatrix} = \\begin{bmatrix} 1 - \\lambda & 2 \\\\ 2 & 1 - \\lambda \\end{bmatrix}\n\\]\n\nNext, we need to find the determinant of this matrix:\n\n\\[\n\\det(A - \\lambda I) = \\det \\begin{bmatrix} 1 - \\lambda & 2 \\\\ 2 & 1 - \\lambda \\end{bmatrix}\n\\]\n\nThe determinant of a 2x2 matrix \\(\\begin{bmatrix} a & b \\\\ c & d \\end{bmatrix}\\) is calculated as \\( ad - bc \\). So,\n\n\\[\n\\det \\begin{bmatrix} 1 - \\lambda & 2 \\\\ 2 & 1 - \\lambda \\end{bmatrix} = (1 - \\lambda)(1 - \\lambda) - (2)(2)\n\\]\n\nSimplify the expression:\n\n\\[\n(1 - \\lambda)^2 - 4 = 0\n\\]\n\nExpanding and simplifying further:\n\n\\[\n1 - 2\\lambda + \\lambda^2 - 4 = 0\n\\]\n\n\\[\n\\lambda^2 - 2\\lambda - 3 = 0\n\\]\n\nThis is a quadratic equation. To solve for \\(\\lambda\\), we use the quadratic formula:\n\n\\[\n\\lambda = \\frac{-b \\pm \\sqrt{b^2 - 4ac}}{2a}\n\\]\n\nHere, \\( a = 1 \\), \\( b = -2 \\), and \\( c = -3 \\). Plugging these values into the quadratic formula:\n\n\\[\n\\lambda = \\frac{-(-2) \\pm \\sqrt{(-2)^2 - 4(1)(-3)}}{2(1)}\n\\]\n\n\\[\n\\lambda = \\frac{2 \\pm \\sqrt{4 + 12}}{2}\n\\]\n\n\\[\n\\lambda = \\frac{2 \\pm \\sqrt{16}}{2}\n\\]\n\n\\[\n\\lambda = \\frac{2 \\pm 4}{2}\n\\]\n\nThis gives us two solutions:\n\n\\[\n\\lambda_1 = \\frac{2 + 4}{2} = 3\n\\]\n\n\\[\n\\lambda_2 = \\frac{2 - 4}{2} = -1\n\\]\n\nSo, the eigenvalues are \\(\\lambda_1 = 3\\) and \\(\\lambda_2 = -1\\).\n\n### Step 2: Find the Eigenvectors\n\nFor each eigenvalue, we find the corresponding eigenvector by solving the equation:\n\n\\[\n(A - 3 \\end{bmatrix} = \\begin{bmatrix} -2 & 2 \\\\ 2 & -2 \\end{bmatrix}\n\\]\n\nWe need to solve:\n\n\\[\n\\begin{bmatrix} -2 & 2 \\\\ 2 & -2 \\end{bmatrix} \\begin{bmatrix} x \\\\ y \\end{bmatrix} = \\begin{bmatrix} 0 \\\\ 0 \\end{bmatrix}\n\\]\n\nThis gives us the system of equations:\n\n\\[\n-2x + 2y = 0\n\\]\n\n\\[\n2x - 2y = 0\n\\]\n\nBoth equations simplify to:\n\n\\[\nx = y\n\\]\n\nSo, the eigenvector corresponding to \\(\\lambda_1 = 3\\) can be written as:\n\n\\[\n\\mathbf{v}_1 = \\begin{bmatrix} x \\\\ x \\end{bmatrix} = x \\begin{bmatrix} 1 \\\\ 1 \\end{bmatrix}\n\\]\n\nWe can choose \\( x = 1 \\) for simplicity, giving us:\n\n\\[\n\\mathbf{v}_1 = \\begin{bmatrix} 1 \\\\ 1 \\end{bmatrix}\n\\]\n\n#### Eigenvector for \\(\\lambda_2 = -1\\)\n\nSubstitute \\(\\lambda = -1\\) into \\( A -
